The Newcomer
2026-07-27

The Newcomer

The Atlantic | 2026-07-27

Visit Original Website
Read more about the Democrats who might run for president in 2028 here. Governor Wes Moore doesn’t care about saving the Democratic Party; if anything, he thinks it has stood in his way. When we met up in May, he quoted Jay-Z: “Industry shady, it need to be taken over.” The 2001 track is about Jay-Z’s rise to fame, and the ways the recording industry tried to exploit and co-opt him because he was “raisin’ the status quo up.” Moore was talking about a failed political system, and a Democratic Party that, he told me, “tried as hard as they could to get me not to run.” Jay-Z describes himself as a “renegade,” and Moore would like to be seen in a similar light—as the outsider who got himself to the top. Moore is the first Black governor of Maryland and the only Black governor serving in America today. At 47, he’s a relative newcomer to politics—he was the head of Robin Hood, a poverty-fighting foundation, until 2021—but he’s already being discussed as a possible presidential candidate in 2028. He’s just the kind of Bronze Star–winning, God-praising, son-of-an-immigrant Rhodes Scholar whom many Democratic voters would see as a dream candidate (although he denied to me that he was considering running). But so far, his tenure as governor has been far from smooth. In May 2025, he surprised many people by vetoing a bill establishing a commission to study the economic effects of slavery and racism on the state’s citizens and to recommend policies that could include reparations. Black legislators were outraged, and the Maryland General Assembly eventually overrode his veto. Moore defended his decision by dismissing the commission as a body conducting mere academic research, when what people needed was real change: “I am not a believer in studies, commissions,” he told me. “I’m like, Are we doing the work or not?” He pointed to big investments in HBCUs and a mass pardon for cannabis convictions as proof of his progressive accomplishments. Richard D. Kahlenberg: The Democrats can’t let go of racial preferences Moore likes to portray himself as an independent actor, beholden to no one. He has signed legislation that prevents 16- and 17-year-olds from being tried as adults for some crimes, and also increased funding for the police. He has, he boasted to me, vetoed “more bills than any Democratic governor in the history of the state of Maryland” because he doesn’t believe in passing “unfunded mandates.” And he doesn’t care whether the party likes it or not: “I didn’t win because of the Democratic Party,” he said. “I won in spite of the Democratic Party.” He added: “I don’t come from nothing, and I didn’t need nothing, and I’m not owned by anybody. When I’m making my decisions, there is no party boss that I call.” Although this message seems tailor-made for this moment, in which so many Americans are simultaneously highly partisan yet disgusted by both parties, Moore’s approval rating in Maryland has fallen below 50 percent for the first time. (He’s still expected to win easily in the November election—he won 88 percent of the vote in the June primary, and one of his staffers shared with me an internal poll that found a higher approval rating.) He’s facing a number of challenges, particularly the dogged perception that he lacks authenticity—that he hasn’t been honest about who he really is. Moore has a politician’s powder-white smile and a bald head that shines like glass. I met him this spring, in Selma, Alabama, where he’d gone to commemorate the anniversary of Bloody Sunday, when about 600 civil-rights marchers were attacked on the Edmund Pettus Bridge. He began the weekend with a speech at Brown Chapel AME Church. “I know we are built for this, because I know what we are built of,” he told the mostly Black crowd. He listed heroes of the civil-rights movement and then said, “This is our time,” holding up his index finger like a Baptist preacher. Everywhere he went, lines formed. And he played his role perfectly. A bear hug for Governor J. B. Pritzker of Illinois. A selfie for a Black teenager with braces. “Good morning! Good morning! Good morning! How’s everybody doin’?” Then, pulling on the words like a southerner: “Alllll riiiiiight.” An hour or so later, at the Historic Tabernacle Baptist Church, his words tipped toward preaching: “I know where my worthiness comes from, and it does not come from Washington, D.C.” A few weeks later, on the Baltimore Orioles’ Opening Day, he stepped out of a black SUV wearing an orange jersey with his name on the back, and a man rushed up to him: “Sign my baseball for when you run for president.” “Not going to happen,” Moore quickly responded—while signing his name. Over the next few months, I watched Moore go from boat ride to baseball game to matcha tasting to college-graduation speech. In every situation, he knew exactly who to be. By the time he quoted Jay-Z to me, I understood that he had me pegged, too. I was fascinated by Moore’s ability to adapt to his different audiences. I grew up having to do the same, and although I appreciate that I can connect with people from both elite and underprivileged backgrounds, I wish I were as much of a natural as he is. Even as a journalist at The Atlantic, I still find myself sometimes intimidated, my voice trembling. Moore moved like water. But this may be one reason some voters struggle to trust him. When I asked people I know in Maryland, many of them Black, what they think of the governor, I heard things such as He doesn’t say what he means and We don’t know what his agenda is. They thought it was weird that he won without having any political experience. That distrust has been fueled by persistent negative reporting from conservative media outlets. At the Orioles game, a white man in a USA shirt said, “I appreciate you, Gov,” as Moore autographed his collection of baseball cards. Then Moore walked onto the field, and many people in the stadium booed. He smiled and clapped for himself, and seemed to take no notice of the crowd’s displeasure. Moore has been accused of making what The Washington Free Beacon called “a litany of false statements about his family history, his upbringing, and his academic, athletic, and military achievements.” The accusations range from minor to significant, and begin with his hometown. The text on the back cover of his 2010 book, The Other Wes Moore: One Name, Two Fates, reads: “Two kids named Wes Moore were born blocks apart.” Both “grew up fatherless in similar Baltimore neighborhoods and had difficult childhoods.” In fact, Moore’s first years were spent in Takoma Park, Maryland—a nice suburb of Washington, which I know because I live about five minutes away. The book itself does mention that his family home was on “the border of Maryland and Washington, D.C.” But many people who read the book still come away with the impression that Moore was born and raised in Baltimore. It’s true that he grew up fatherless. Moore was 3 when his father died of a virus; he believes that a racist health-care system gave him shoddy treatment. His mother moved with Moore and his sisters to the Bronx, to be closer to her family. He attended the elite Riverdale Country School, but after his grades dropped and he got caught with a smoke bomb in the locker room as a prank, his mother sent him to Valley Forge Military Academy, in Pennsylvania. (His grandparents took out a second mortgage to help pay the tuition.) He moved back to Baltimore only as a college student, to go to Johns Hopkins University. He was living there in 2015 when the city erupted in protests following the death of Freddie Gray, who suffered a spinal-cord injury while inside a police van. Moore co-authored an account of that period with Erica L. Green, Five Days: The Fiery Reckoning of an American City. (“I am from Baltimore,” he wrote in that book.) He later kept a home in the city while commuting to Robin Hood in New York. After his Baltimore bona fides came under scrutiny in 2022, his campaign said that he had asked his publisher to correct the promotional text on The Other Wes Moore, and it did for later editions. Moore quickly learned to be more careful: “I’m not a Baltimorean by birth, but I’m a Baltimorean by choice,” he told a crowd in 2022. But a pattern had been established—or at least a line of attack. In 2010, Stephen Colbert described Moore, a guest on The Colbert Report, as a recipient of a Bronze Star for his service in Afghanistan, where he’d served in 2005 and 2006. The trouble was that he didn’t have a Bronze Star. When reporters raised questions about the medal, Moore’s staff insisted that he’d never told anyone that he had won it, and so he wasn’t responsible for the error. But in 2024, The New York Times obtained Moore’s application for a White House fellowship in 2006 in which he’d written, “the 82nd Airborne Division have awarded me the Bronze Star Medal.” He had an explanation for that too: The senior officer who had helped Moore write the application told him that he was going to get the medal, and so he should include it; the fact that it never actually arrived was embarrassing, but not unusual in the chaos of wartime. And after the controversy surfaced, the military did indeed award him a Bronze Star—just 18 years late. From the July 2026 issue: Being Black in Pete Hegseth’s military Then the Free Beacon ran a story questioning Moore’s account that his great-grandfather had moved to Jamaica because he’d been targeted by the KKK, based on the fact that reporters couldn’t find contemporaneous newspaper or archival accounts to verify it. This charge seemed more than a little forced—as did the many stories about Moore’s thesis at the University of Oxford. In that same White House fellowship application, he wrote that he’d received his degree in 2003 or 2004, but it wasn’t formally recorded until 2005. He also listed the title of his thesis as “The Rise and Ramifications of Radical Islam in the Western Hemisphere,” but it was later titled “Radical Islam in Latin America in the Late 20th Century and Its Middle Eastern Roots.” Also, the thesis is not stored in Oxford’s Bodleian Library—though there is no requirement for theses to be kept there, and many aren’t. Moore told The Baltimore Sun that he wasn’t “going to spend a second of my time trying to dig” a copy up. If you’re a little bored, you’re right to be. Some people have compared Moore to Barack Obama, and the demands for him to release more records to back up his past inevitably reminded me of the “birther” conspiracy. At least that conspiracy had some stakes. How much should anyone care that in 2006, Moore said he’d been named to the Maryland College Football Hall of Fame when in fact he had been honored only by a College Football Hall of Fame affiliate called the National Football Foundation? Moore expresses regret for not being clear about the Bronze Star, but when I asked him about the attacks on his truthfulness in general, he dismissed them. “Do you know who has never attacked my military record? The United States Army,” he said. He told me he lived by a code: “You do not lie, cheat, or steal.” In fact, he said, he was being accused of dishonesty precisely because he was so honest. “It’s basic laws of warfare: You go after your opponent’s strength.” And he pointed out that these accusations surfaced only after he started gaining support. “I went a whole year on a campaign, and no one had a word to say about me,” he said. “And then out of nowhere, it was like the deluge began,” and people from “the right wing started putting stuff out.” The Baltimore Sun, which has devoted much space to these controversies, has indeed moved rightward under a new owner, the television magnate David Smith, and the conservative Free Beacon’s tagline is a promise to cover “the enemies of freedom in the way the mainstream media won’t.” Moore told me that Obama had given him advice on surviving these kinds of attacks as a politician, saying, “Listen, this is the part of the game that everyone hates.” But if the questions about Moore’s honesty have been amplified by his opponents, they still trace back to an uncomfortable set of facts: Moore allowed people to call him a Bronze Star–winning Baltimore kid, when the story was more complicated. In April, one of Moore’s biggest legislative priorities—a redistricting plan that could have helped Democrats pick up a seat in the midterms—died when William Ferguson, the Democratic president of the state Senate, refused to bring it up for a vote. It was “too risky,” Ferguson argued: The new map could be challenged, and a ruling could leave their party worse off. Ferguson is now pushing to get a constitutional amendment before Maryland voters in November that would better shore up any new map from legal challenges. Moore has framed the legislature’s caution as timidity, but Ferguson has a different take. He told me that Moore had created confusion by waiting months before coming out in favor of the plan, and suggested that the governor embraced it only after Gavin Newsom gained national attention for pushing through a new map in California. “I don’t think that timing was a coincidence,” Ferguson said, adding that Moore tends to emphasize “messaging” over governing. “Comms is dictating policy, and policy is not dictating comms.” He did, however, credit Moore with working to speedily rebuild the Francis Scott Key Bridge. Its collapse, in 2024, killed six people and is the most significant catastrophe Moore has weathered as governor. The port of Baltimore was expected to be shut down for many months, but the state got it open again after less than three. Moore is expected to win a second term in November, but the public’s mistrust has taken a political toll. The Sun and others have accused him of not being forthcoming about the state’s budget, which has seesawed from deficit to surplus to deficit again during his tenure. His vetoing of the reparations bill made some of his core supporters feel that he had misled them. When Moore’s approval rating declined, poll respondents listed the budget and the governor’s trustworthiness among their top concerns. Something about Moore does feel protean. He’s hard to place. His background can be confusing. Although he’s often compared to Obama, his education was far less rarefied. Obama was the president of the Harvard Law Review; Moore went to junior college before landing at Johns Hopkins. He describes his upbringing as working-class—“a very hardworking family, but it was definitely a working-class family.” Perhaps by income, but by few other definitions. Both parents were college graduates. His father was an anchorman at a radio station and his mother wrote for radio and TV. Although Moore frequently says that she didn’t have a job with benefits until he was a teenager, she had won a Peabody Award and taught journalism at Howard University before he was born. Still, he saw himself as an outsider, and wouldn’t invite his friends from Riverdale back to his neighborhood. He told me that he thought very strategically about fitting in: “Okay, how do I adapt and adjust so I don’t stick out?” At Valley Forge, he was taught how to mingle with the powerful—how to walk, eat, and speak. As a high-ranking cadet, he attended lunches with former presidents, including Gerald Ford and George H. W. Bush. He said he eventually learned to accept that he was different and to be “comfortable in my own skin.” In two separate conversations, he quoted the same Jay-Z line: “I’m comfortable, dog, Brooklyn to Rome.” But he enunciated every word, in a way that sounded stiff. Despite his charisma onstage, up close, he has no swag. Where he seemed most at home was at the matcha café in Baltimore owned by an old friend of his, who surprised him with a Jamaican sorrel—a tart iced drink. “This right here would make my grandmother proud,” he said. Once, I made a Bernie Mac reference, and he got it immediately, and we shared a knowing laugh. Above all, he was comfortable talking about religion. “I come from a whole long line of preachers and teachers,” he said. “My great-grandfather was a minister; my grandfather was a minister.” In the summers, he used to stay in his grandfather’s parsonage in Brooklyn. When he told his grandmother that he was running for office, she said, “When you’re done with that, maybe you can become a minister.” “Asking me not to talk about or to acknowledge God is like asking me not to breathe air,” he told me. When I asked his mother, Joy Moore, if she thought he would run for president, she said: “I think his faith will take him wherever he’s going to eventually go.” She seemed more annoyed by the questions about his honesty and integrity than Moore was. “They question things that are so personal,” she said. She felt as if the articles interrogating their family history were accusing them all—her grandfather, her father, herself, her son—of being liars. And the Oxford thesis? “I mean, if he knew he was going to one day run for governor, don’t you think he would have kept stuff like that?” Read: Something is happening in the Democratic base Moore says he’d had no plans to go into politics before 2022. It was his wife, Dawn, who was the political operative, a campaign strategist who’d worked for two lieutenant governors. She and Moore eloped when he was on leave from his service in Afghanistan. While they were dating, she was diagnosed with multiple sclerosis, and I was curious about whether she found divulging it to him hard. After all, I know plenty of men who wouldn’t stick around for the common cold. She said she asked him: “How would you feel if you had to take care of me for the rest of your life?” And he said: “How would you feel, if you had to take care of me for the rest of my life?” Some of the questions regarding Moore’s authenticity amount to a nebulous discomfort with how he comes across, a discomfort I recognize from all the times I’ve encountered it myself. Many people who work in elite careers after growing up relatively disadvantaged, as well as Black people who spend time in primarily white spaces, are accused of not being true to themselves. This is a false dichotomy. We can be true to ourselves while also understanding that success requires us to be supple and adaptable, and to present different aspects of our personalities at different times. There’s a famous poem by Paul Laurence Dunbar called “We Wear the Mask,” about the way Black Americans shield themselves from racism. I mentioned it to Moore, and he recognized it immediately. “The mask is heavy,” he said. Moore says he wears his own mask around many people, “not because it makes me more comfortable—it’s because it makes them more comfortable.” Being in spaces where people are “not used to seeing you” is a burden, he said. Being “an enigma” is a burden. “To some people, your presence is inspirational. To certain people, your presence is offensive. To certain people, your presence means progress. To certain people, your presence means we’ve lost something.” You’re a first, or you’re a threat. “That weight does sit on you,” he said. “And it’s heavy.”

Odysseus’s Confession
2026-07-26

Odysseus’s Confession

The Atlantic | 2026-07-26

Visit Original Website
Toward the end of Homer’s Odyssey, Odysseus and Penelope finally see each other after 20 years apart. Odysseus, disguised as “an ancient old man” by the goddess Athena, tells Penelope that her husband will return to kill the suitors who have tormented her. “Not one will avoid his death and destruction,” he says. This is Homer’s protagonist: wily, aided by a god, and eager for revenge—reflective of a culture that prized honor, strength, and fealty to a set of interventionist deities. This is not, however, Christopher Nolan’s protagonist. At key moments in the recent blockbuster adaptation of the ancient-Greek poem, Odysseus’s story seems more biblical than Homeric. In the film, when Odysseus reunites with Penelope, he hasn’t been turned into an old man. Nolan keeps his identity secret through more naturalistic means: a dimly lit room, a cloak over his body, and latticed sliding doors that separate the two characters. The doors, which are not found in Homer’s poem, are evocative of the screen between priest and penitent in Catholic confession—a setting that Nolan, who was raised Catholic, would be familiar with. And although in Homer’s poem, Odysseus is thirsty for retribution, Nolan emphasizes a different emotion in this scene, one that is integral to the confessional and to Christian life as a whole: contrition. Odysseus describes deep guilt over his central role in a famous episode of the Trojan War: when Greek soldiers hid inside a wooden horse to trick the people of Troy into letting them into their city. “We left them a gift,” he tells his wife, “an offering of peace, that they took into their home.” In sacking Troy, Odysseus says, he and his fellow soldiers “violated all that’s ever sacred between people”—he recognizes a link between mistreating humanity and disrespecting divinity. He confesses that he “lost his way,” not just temporally or physically but in his soul. Other aspects of Nolan’s adaptation feel biblical, even Christian, rather than Homeric—a quasi-baptism scene at sea, for example, and the conspicuous absence of the references to sex that populate the poem. The core principle of the film’s moral universe seems to echo the Bible. “Treat others as you would be treated,” Penelope declares early in the film, expressing an ethic that various characters refer to as “Zeus’s law.” Many versions of the idea exist, in secular and religious forms. But in the film, Penelope’s words sound more like Jesus’s Golden Rule than anything in Homer, where the heroes—though bound to host strangers as if they might be gods in disguise—seek to achieve glory more than convey empathy. Read: Reading a 3,000-year-old poem to a 3-year-old boy The film’s treatment of Odysseus’s lies, too, seems consonant with the Ten Commandments instead of Homer’s moral worldview. In the poem, Odysseus’s lies are often portrayed positively, as evidence of his intelligence and resourcefulness—when he tricks the Cyclops, for example. In the film, however, Odysseus’s falsehoods have dire consequences, an apparent reflection of the biblical assertion that false witness is a sin. Odysseus tasks a young soldier named Sinon—a character who does not appear in the poem—with offering the horse to the Trojans. But he doesn’t tell Sinon that its purpose is to attack the city. Sinon dies as a result of Odysseus’s deception. Later, in Hades, he admonishes his commander: “Did you have to lie to me?” So did Christopher Nolan make a Christian film, which the Daily Wire founder Ben Shapiro and others have suggested since the movie’s release? Not quite. As my colleague Gal Beckerman wrote, Nolan diminishes the role of the gods, treating them as socially useful but not necessarily real. It’s hard to call definitively “Christian” a movie that regards the divine as a possible fantasy. The film is God-haunted, though. Homer may have viewed Odysseus’s deceit and pillaging as glorious. Nolan’s adaptation seems to pass a different judgment. Animated in part by the wisdom of the Bible, this modern Odyssey can’t help but see how the deceptions of war degrade a person and a culture.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine
We Faced Down Trump’s Secret Police in Minneapolis. Here’s How We Did It.
2026-07-27

We Faced Down Trump’s Secret Police in Minneapolis. Here’s How We Did It.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-07-27

Visit Original Website
This is part of Amicus and Slate’s “By the People” series, which explores how the American people can reclaim the Constitution—and the law—from this monarchical Supreme Court. When Tippy Amundson was in middle school, she read the diary of a teenager hiding from the secret police. “Terrible things are happening outside,” it read. “Families are torn apart; men, women, and children are separated. Children come home from school to find that their parents have disappeared.” Amundson tried to imagine what she would do if she lived during such terrible times. I would totally hide Anne Frank, she reasoned. But when would you ever have the opportunity to do that? This is a civilized world we live in now. This is America. A quarter century later, Amundson, now a 39-year-old travel agent and former kindergarten teacher, received a call from her 13-year-old neighbor. He asked if he could hide in her house if Immigration and Customs Enforcement officers chased him home from his bus stop. It was then she knew that the time had come. Amundson’s teenage neighbor had been considering what he might do if he found himself in ICE’s crosshairs. His parents didn’t want him to go to school; they themselves were afraid to go to work. Every day when he got off the bus, he looked around for unmarked cars filled with masked agents wearing body armor and carrying guns, afraid that they might ask him for his papers or put him in their SUVs and drive away. As he walked up to the door, he checked over his shoulders to see if he was being watched, and he hoped his parents would still be waiting for him inside. Amundson agreed to watch the bus stop when he got home and to hide him if necessary. Their story, which only got more dramatic from there, is a prime example of how the people of Minneapolis responded to unprecedented state-sanctioned lawlessness: They mobilized en masse to protect their neighbors. Early in Donald Trump’s second term, the White House issued mandates of ICE and Border Patrol to arrest 3,000 people a day. Lawmakers were denied entry when they tried to perform their mandated oversight duties at immigrant detention facilities. ICE’s mission broadened to police those opposed to the president. The Department of Homeland Security began to surveil social media posts of Trump’s political opponents, using facial recognition software to create “terrorist” databases of peaceful protesters and following the demonstrators to their houses, sometimes calling them by name. ICE and Border Patrol agents, in masks and unwilling to identify themselves, were empowered by the Supreme Court to demand the papers of people because of their accent, their job, or simply their “apparent race or ethnicity.” In short, according to Lee Morgenbesser, who studies past and present authoritarian regimes and secret police, ICE meets the criteria for a secret police force. Secret police, or something very near that description, swept through Chicago from September to December 2025. Senior Border Patrol commander Greg Bovino, with masked and militarized federal officers, waged a monthslong rampage that saw protesters, first responders, and children tear-gassed, journalists arrested, citizens abducted and shot, and a man killed. It was then that the Trump administration settled its gaze on Minneapolis. A haven for refugees fleeing a civil war in the late ’90s, Minnesota is home to some 80,000 people of Somali descent, most of whom are citizens. “I don’t want them in this country, to be honest with you,” Trump said to his Cabinet and reporters about Americans of Somali descent. “We’re at a tipping point. I don’t know if people mind me saying that, but I’m saying it. We could go one way or the other, and we’re going to go the wrong way if we keep taking in garbage to our country.” On Dec. 2, federal police kidnapped a Metro Transit bus driver on his way to work because he was Somali. The officers unlawfully detained him (or, more accurately, trafficked him) in three different states, and he wouldn’t see his family again for a month. So began Operation Metro Surge, an invasion whose name was inspired by the war on terror and which was conceived to punish a place where Trump is deeply unpopular. “THE DAY OF RECKONING AND RETRIBUTION IS COMING,” the president tweeted in all caps at the height of the occupation. “My kids were literally pointing out masked gunmen on the way to school,” Amundson remembered. Agents with their faces covered up to their eyes, in unmarked black SUVs with tinted windows, would wait outside churches, mosques, schools, daycares, courtrooms, and hospitals. They would sweep neighborhoods where they thought immigrants lived, cornering people alone, rolling up on them and throwing them into their vehicles for spot citizenship checks. Refugees, asylum-seekers with pending applications, green-card holders, citizens, and children alike were swept off the streets and pulled into detention. When I left for work during that period, I would see legal observers in high-vis vests standing outside the school across from my house, keeping watch and signaling to students that they were safe and that they belonged. I drove to my job at a middle school, where more legal observers patrolled outside. In the relative safety inside, attendance plummeted among Black and brown students. Those who did come to school told me about the eyes of masked agents staring at them as they had left their apartments that morning, or the unmarked black SUVs that had followed them on their bus routes the day before to see where the brown kids got off, or the nightmares they had of ICE agents breaking down their doors and taking away their parents and siblings. Some families hid in basements, parents telling their children to avoid going to the first floor at all in case they were spotted through a window. It all came to a head when Renee Nicole Good, a mother and poet two years younger than Amundson, was shot in her car after dropping her 6-year-old off at school. When her wife got out of the car to film and confront ICE officers, Good was told to leave, then ordered to get out of the car. Confused, she started to drive away. That’s when agent Jonathan Ross moved in front of her car and shot her three times: in her arm, chest, and head. According to an independent autopsy, the shots to her arm and chest had not been life-threatening, but the one to her temple had. “Fucking bitch,” Ross said, as the car careered into a pole. Good remained alive for minutes afterward. A bystander begged to help Good, to check her pulse and perform first aid. “I’m a physician!” “Don’t care!” another agent responded. This retort was familiar to Minneapolis residents. It was the message sent by local police officers who refused to let an off-duty firefighter help as officer Derek Chauvin murdered George Floyd. Good was still alive by the time paramedics arrived, but they were unable to save her life. Her family has confirmed that Minneapolis authorities were finally granted access this month to the evidence in her killing, which will allow them to conduct a proper investigation. When Floyd was killed, Minneapolitans were furious. But when Good was killed, our anger was tinged with fear. Many people retreated inside. For some, though, this only strengthened their resolve to monitor agents to make sure they didn’t kill others. Three days after Good’s death, Amundson drove past a Latino grocery store in her neighborhood and saw a group of people sitting in front of the entrance. She pulled over to ask them what was going on. “We think there’s an ICE agent,” someone told her, pointing toward an unmarked car skulking in the parking lot. Amundson went inside the store, where 20 people were hiding. She volunteered to walk them out to their cars, using her white body as a kind of shield. In the parking lot, a legal observer helped her download the encrypted messaging app Signal and join her neighborhood rapid-response group. In the Twin Cities, neighbors tend to know one another, either from one of the 2,000 block parties held during the summer for National Night Out (the event boasts one of the highest attendances in the country), from volunteer work (again, Minnesota has one of the highest rates of any state), or from the logistical coordination required to move snow and ice from public sidewalks, a civic responsibility most Minnesotans take very seriously. This was a fertile environment for the proliferation of digital rapid-response networks at a time when they were most needed. Using Signal, neighbors alerted one another when agents were near. There were large groups that covered swaths of the Twin Cities (all of South Minneapolis, for example), while hyperlocal group chats and voice calls tracked specific neighborhoods. Legal observers, volunteers who sought to surveil the activity of federal agents to ensure safety and compliance with the law, as well as to document who was being taken, came outside when alerted to their presence and blew whistles to warn those nearby. About 30,000 Minnesotans were trained as observers, all intent on making sure that the human beings around them were being treated with basic dignity and given due process. The same week as her trip to the grocery store, Amundson was driving when she saw six masked officers jump out of a car and swarm a man sitting alone at a bus stop. They forced him to empty his pockets and demanded to see his papers. Amundson stopped, got out of her car, and filmed them. “What was I thinking?” she reflected later. How was a “former kindergarten teacher, mom of three, going to take on these masked gunmen?” But she did it anyway. “We’re all trying to do what we can to make these people that we care about feel safe.” Amundson wasn’t an expert on the law, but she knew right from wrong. She was horrified that children as young as 2 were being imprisoned in camps where food is crawling with maggots, where the putrid water makes people sick, where dormant diseases like measles and tuberculosis spread, and where, more frequently than ever, people die. “This is my neighbor,” Amundson explained simply. Atlantic writer Adam Serwer describes what happened in response to Operation Metro Surge as “neighborism,” the ideology that one should care for, and protect, one’s surrounding communities. To me, it captures how people protect one another when the law and its appointed enforcers cannot be trusted. “Who keeps us safe?” began a popular call-and-response protest chant I first heard during the George Floyd uprising and that resurfaced during Metro Surge. “We keep us safe.” On Jan. 22, Amundson and her neighbor Heather Zemien were driving together, patrolling their neighborhood and ensuring that kids were getting home safely after school, when they saw masked agents with guns clustered near the area where kids would soon be getting dropped off by buses. They honked the horn nine times to alert others nearby. The agents approached their car and warned them to stop. Zemien and Amundson complied and had started to exit the parking lot when a local police officer drove in. They pulled over and briefly asked the officer if she had been called in because of them. The officer told them no. “Do I recommend not getting involved? Yes,” she added, abdicating all responsibility for the safety of the neighborhood. “We’re getting involved. They’re our neighbors,” Amundson responded nonconfrontationally. “If ICE shows up to my house because they think neighbors are inside my house, should I call you?” Amundson went on to ask, the question ever present in her mind. Who keeps us safe? But before the officer could elucidate a satisfying response, agents drove up and surrounded Zemien and Amundson’s vehicle. They asked the women if they had called the police, then ripped them out of the car into the subzero air and put handcuffs around their wrists. As they did so, another masked agent in tactical gear filmed them with a professional camera. The police officer watched Zemien and Amundson be taken away in an unmarked car. As they were being driven off, the women noticed that something was wrong with the agent in the front passenger seat. “I’m not feeling so well,” he said, before sliding down and convulsing. The other young agents were frozen, unsure what to do. “He’s having a seizure,” Amundson said. At first they ignored her. “Call fucking 911!” Zemien instructed them. Finally, they pulled over. “He needs help. I have some medical training,” Amundson told them. “You need to uncuff me so I can put him in the recovery position.” They did. Amundson, now uncuffed, walked to the side of the seizing agent, a man who had terrorized her own children. She eased the seat back and took the gun from his hip so he could lie on his side. His mask had slid off, and Amundson was taken aback by how young he was. She began soothing him. Zemien, who had also been uncuffed, started digging through the SUV, collecting spare tactical vests, then propped them underneath the agent’s head. “Hi, my name’s Tippy,” Amundson said. “What’s your name?” He told her. “This was between seizures,” she told me later. “I watched him have three seizures. That ICE agent could have choked on his tongue had I not been there.” In front of her was a human in need. The people nearby didn’t know what to do, so she did what she thought anybody would: She helped him. Eventually, the young agent was taken away by paramedics. Amundson and Zemien’s hopes that their behavior would warrant some leniency were quickly dashed. They were handcuffed again and taken to the Whipple Federal Building. “Because you were so nice to us, we’ll give you your phone call,” the secret police told the women as they drove them to the site where ICE agents were detaining citizens and noncitizens alike. They were charged with obstruction. (Eventually, those charges were dropped.) As they left, their citations in hand, an agent in charge said, “Tell everyone how nice we were to you.” Two days after Amundson’s arrest, ICE agents on the streets of Minneapolis shot Alex Pretti 10 times in the back. Pretti’s last act had been to try to defend someone from pepper spray. Amundson watched the video, and what struck her most was that another agent started clapping and laughing after the 10 shots were fired. Even after the second killing in a month, Amundson continued to observe her neighbor getting off the bus. As civil resistance grew, ICE agents were eventually forced to change their approach from military occupation to a new phase that relied on covert tactics and the collaboration of local law enforcement. People kept disappearing, but schools again filled with traumatized students. “The kids are playing again, and it feels lighter,” Amundson told me months later. “How wonderful it is that nobody need wait a single moment before starting to improve the world,” a 15-year-old in Nazi Germany marveled, before she was taken by the secret police to a concentration camp and killed. “It’s really a wonder that I haven’t dropped all my ideals, because they seem so absurd and impossible to carry out. Yet I keep them, because in spite of everything, I still believe that people are really good at heart.” In the week after my own detainment by the secret police, I took time off work because I was afraid to leave the house. Out the window, I noticed a spot in the alley where the thawing and freezing of ice had created a ravine, where car tires would get lodged and trap the driver. Three separate times, cars got stuck. Hearing the futile spinning of tires, neighbors came out en masse to figure out how to solve the problem. People sprinkled cat litter, got mats out of their own vehicles to place under the tires. Once, someone laid down a broken door, which they had stored in their basement, underneath a car. Despite my fear, I got out of the house. I knocked on the doors of more neighbors to come out and push together. We did what we could with the resources we had, because being a neighbor means watching out for one another. What do you do when the police are doing roving citizen checks on your brown neighbors? When constitutional rights are suspended and the orders of judges ignored by secret police? When doors are battered in without judicial warrants? When citizens are dragged out into the street in their boxers? When children are put in camps? When people are shot in the street for the crime of compassion? You find your neighbors, go outside, and push. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Minneapolis Just Showed the Entire Country How to Take the Constitution Back
2026-07-27

Minneapolis Just Showed the Entire Country How to Take the Constitution Back

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-07-27

Visit Original Website
This is part of Amicus and Slate’s “By the People” series, which explores how the American people can reclaim the Constitution—and the law—from this monarchical Supreme Court. This January, a neighbor texted me a video, along with a warning to avoid our neighborhood. The video showed dozens of people following a black SUV past my home in Minneapolis, blowing whistles and shouting. It was not quite a protest chant, but a verbal alarm for the neighborhood. People came to windows, porches, and icy sidewalks in below-freezing weather because someone had seen Immigration and Customs Enforcement agents in the vehicle, and the new ground rule was simple: None of our neighbors should be taken by ICE without witnesses. This scene captured a common neighborhood response during Operation Metro Surge. There were whistles, Signal chats, legal observers, carpools, rent funds, and grocery deliveries. Warnings passed from block to block, orally and digitally. Some parents kept children home; other children insisted that their parents stay home instead. Residents relayed when not to drive, when not to return, when to stay inside, and when to come out in force. My experience was not exceptional, and that was the point. The surge made public safety a daily practice for many residents. None of it sounded like law in a traditional register. Rather, it was accompaniment: feeding, sheltering, driving, watching, and warning so no one had to move in isolation. What we saw in Minneapolis this past winter was, among other extraordinary things, popular constitutionalism in action. Popular constitutionalism usually names the idea that constitutional meaning is not the province of courts alone. Movements argue about the Constitution, organize around it, and sometimes make claims powerful enough that courts later recognize them as law. Operation Metro Surge belongs to that tradition, but at a more immediate and less familiar level. Residents were not waiting for judicial recognition. They were not claiming the state’s police power for themselves but acting as though they already had a legitimate claim to ensuring the entire community’s safety. The state of Minnesota translated these safety claims into a federal lawsuit. Along with Minneapolis and St. Paul, it challenged Operation Metro Surge by arguing that the federal government had disrupted schools, businesses, emergency services, municipal resources, streets, and the trust on which local governance depends. The complaint invoked Minnesota’s “inviolable sovereign authority” and its duty to ensure the “health, education, and safety” of residents. Translated into constitutional terms, Minnesota argued that the surge violated federalist principles by invading the state’s reserved police powers: the broad authority to govern public health, safety, welfare, and order. That claim translated the fear and disruption of the surge into a constitutional injury that courts could recognize. Yet in Minneapolis, that translation carried its own history. This is the city where police murdered George Floyd, and where the resulting uprising remade the national vocabulary of police violence. Here, the promise that local law enforcement keeps people safe is often hard for residents to take at face value. For many in the city, the problem posed by Operation Metro Surge was not simply whether federal agents had displaced local police. It was that public safety had to be routed through either federal or state policing for the claim to become legible. The whistles and the mutual aid networks demonstrated that it did not. Recent reporting on the Minneapolis Police Department’s response to the surge has sharpened this point. Residents called for help or to report masked agents, guns pointed at protestors, the unrestrained use of chemical munitions, and what looked like kidnappings in the street. The city spent tens of millions in police overtime and standby during the surge, while police rarely responded directly to immigration-related emergency calls. Much of the overtime money the department spent went instead toward preparing for unrest, monitoring protests and vigils, guarding hotels housing federal agents, and managing the public response to federal violence. Local police power did not simply fail to protect residents from federal enforcement—it sought to manage and limit residents’ response to that violence. Resident safety practices are not alien to constitutional history. Long before modern professional police forces, public order depended on watches, constables, alarms, and forms of compelled or expected community response. The old common-law “hue and cry” is not a model to romanticize; it could be coercive, punitive, and exclusionary. But it does show that the state’s current monopoly over the legal meaning of public safety is not natural. Residents’ safety practices during Operation Metro Surge did not revive a bygone era, but exposed the contingency of the present one: the modern assumption that public safety becomes legally cognizable only when the state performs it. The practices in this period changed as enforcement changed. A warning system would form around one pattern of ICE activity, and then the pattern would change. A route became dangerous, then known, then avoided. A license plate circulated on chat networks, and agents later swapped it out. Each day, people adjusted to federal movement, ICE and Customs and Border Protection adjusted to being watched, and people adjusted again. This was not insurgency. It was the refusal of pacification. As policing theorist Mark Neocleous has argued, pacification is the production of order by the very forces that create the danger they claim to manage. It makes official violence look calm and inevitable while popular response is made to look like disorder. Operation Metro Surge was announced as a law enforcement campaign, but many residents experienced it as an effort to pacify the city through fear and overwhelming force. Residents refused to play along. The struggle over public safety became concrete when residents began placing makeshift barricades in the streets to slow ICE vehicles. They were formed with cones, shipping pallets, chairs, trash bins, or even neighbors inside a traffic circle. These were small and temporary impediments, but they worked well enough to make a vehicle pause, and to give residents time to check its license plate against a database, see who was inside, and record and relay the information. The city described the barricades through the language of safety as well. When makeshift roadblocks appeared, Minneapolis urged residents to stop erecting them, citing the need for emergency access, fire response, and roadways open for all. City workers cleared them as quickly as they appeared. Of course, these concerns were real. A blocked street can delay an ambulance or endanger someone whose emergency has nothing to do with immigration enforcement. But the city’s response to the barricades did not settle the public safety question. It revealed an assumption in law about who may act in the name of safety. Police may close roads, establish perimeters, kettle crowds, redirect traffic, block movement, and call those acts public order. Residents doing the same acquire different labels: obstruction, disorder, interference, and even conspiracy. Put differently, law does not begin by asking what the barricade does. It first asks who built it. The state may control movement because it bears the police power, and it bears the police power because it is the state. Once that axiom is treated as constitutional common sense, public safety appears already on the side of the enforcement vehicle. This is where Minnesota’s lawsuit and Minneapolis residents’ barricade response pulled against each other. In court, the state and cities argued that Operation Metro Surge had invaded state and municipal authority. In the street, the city sought to reclaim that authority from residents. Public safety could be local rather than federal, but still institutional rather than popular. The popular response to Operation Metro Surge did not resolve that tension by choosing the city over the federal government. It rejected the assumption that both shared: that public safety must remain an institutional monopoly. Of course, popular claims to community safety can become dangerous. They have justified lynching; border vigilantism; racial, gendered, and xenophobic exclusion; neighborhood surveillance; and mob violence. That history is why contesting the constitutional allocation of public safety cannot mean giving private people authority to govern vulnerable people. The question is not whether state power or popular power is always safer. Neither is. The harder question is what each is doing. What power is being exercised, against whom, by what means, and in response to what danger? In Minneapolis, residents perceived the danger not as the vulnerable populations being stopped, but as armed federal power. The means they deployed were not violent resistance, but warning, watching, accompanying, documenting, delaying, material support, and refusing isolation. That is the popular constitutional contest the whistles, warnings, carpools, legal observers, and barricades made visible. Federal agents claimed public safety by conducting dragnet stops. Local officials claimed public safety by managing the response. Residents claimed public safety by slowing that movement and making enforcement visible. Their practices showed that public safety cannot be measured only by who has legal authority to act in its name. Safety “by the people, for the people” then, is not private policing or blind faith in the crowd. It is the insistence that public safety be judged by the people who must live with its consequences. Their Loved Ones Died to Give Us the Voting Rights Act. They Have a Message for the Supreme Court.
2026-07-27

Their Loved Ones Died to Give Us the Voting Rights Act. They Have a Message for the Supreme Court.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-07-27

Visit Original Website
This is part of Amicus and Slate’s “By the People” series, which explores how the American people can reclaim the Constitution—and the law—from this monarchical Supreme Court. Viola Liuzzo, a nurse and mother of five, was not even 40 years old when she was gunned down on the way home from Selma, Alabama, by the Ku Klux Klan after marching across the Edmund Pettus Bridge. Vernon Dahmer had been active in the Civil Rights Movement in Mississippi for years when he was murdered by the klan in a firebombing that nearly killed his entire family. Andrew Goodman was barely 20 when he, James Chaney, and Michael Schwerner were murdered by the KKK as they volunteered to help with voter-registration efforts in rural Mississippi. They are just a few of the most famous martyrs of the movement that gave birth to the 1965 Voting Rights Act. For decades, their families could look back at their sacrifices in sorrow but also in pride at how their lives had forged a true American democracy. This past April, though, something changed. In a 6–3 ruling in Louisiana v. Callais, the Supreme Court’s conservative supermajority killed the last remaining prong of the VRA, the cause for which these heroes had given their last full measure of devotion. “Many paid the ultimate price for Black people to gain the right to vote,” Dennis Dahmer, one of Vernon Dahmer’s sons, told me. “And to see what has systematically been done over the years to that Voting Rights Act, dismantled brick by brick, and finally get this Callais ruling, it’s deeply disappointing.” Liuzzo’s son Anthony was more blunt, saying we have lost the “protections” for our democracy “that my mother’s blood gave us.” Slate spoke with a number of surviving family members of some of the martyrs of the Civil Rights Movement, all of whom were devastated by the court’s Callais ruling. Their stories underscore the travesty of what this Supreme Court has stolen from “we the people” with its yearslong decimation of the VRA. However, they also offer a blueprint for how to restore our democracy and get our country back to a place where the U.S. Constitution can work as it was intended, to offer tangible protections for each and every one of us. The journey will require an impenetrable will and a fearlessness to sacrifice everything for the sake of democracy for all. According to these survivors, though, it will also require something far more basic: going to the ballot box. Start with Viola Liuzzo’s story. In her youth, Liuzzo moved around a lot, but no matter where she lived, whether Pennsylvania or Georgia, Tennessee or Michigan, she couldn’t help but notice that Black Americans were treated differently from how she was, as a white woman. She grew up during segregation, witnessed integration, and came of age when klansmen were part of the fabric of daily life, active in everything from police forces to local governments. She watched Black Americans struggle to exercise their right to vote and knew that there was something deeply wrong with the system. Soon, she became one of the first few white people to join the NAACP. On March 24, 1965, Liuzzo was among the hundreds of protesters who marched across Selma’s Edmund Pettus Bridge to demand equal voting rights. “Bloody Sunday” was also the last time she would speak to her family and friends, or lend her activism to the Civil Rights Movement. “She wasn’t one to stand on the sidelines,” Anthony Liuzzo told me. “She stood there and got involved in the fight.” As activists began their journeys home after the violence inflicted on protesters that day, Viola Liuzzo was individually targeted. A crew of Ku Klux Klan members followed her car as she made the trek back to Michigan, her husband and five children anxiously awaiting her arrival. Eventually, the group pulled up next to her and shot her to death. She was 39 years old. Anthony Liuzzo, who was 10 when his mother died and 71 when SCOTUS ended the last protections of the VRA this April, remembers his mother as someone who was always volunteering herself for the cause of human rights, a nurse by trade who would often bring home sex workers and people with addiction issues to help rehab them and get them off the streets. She would emphasize to her children that all humans were created equal, no matter the color of their skin, Anthony Liuzzo told me, recalling a time she had grabbed his arm and said, “Well, nothing comes off. We’re all the same, just the pigment is different in our skin.” The Liuzzo family learned of Viola Liuzzo’s death in the middle of the night, after the local sheriff’s office called them with the news. “It was madness after she was assassinated. I saw things that no 10-year-old should have to see,” Anthony Liuzzo said. “We had bullets shot through our windows, crosses burned in our yard, garbage dumped in our yard.” The Liuzzo family was harassed for their mother’s activism even after her death, but the event galvanized activists all over the country, becoming one impetus for Congress to finally pass the landmark 1965 legislation. About four months after Viola Liuzzo’s death, President Lyndon Johnson signed the Voting Rights Act into law. Sixty-one years later, on April 29, 2026, Anthony Liuzzo and Americans across the country witnessed six individuals, in one fell swoop, issue a binding opinion in Louisiana v. Callais, dismantling the work that thousands of activists had spilled blood, sweat, and tears over. The court’s far-right majority declared that racism was no longer a problem and that, therefore, Section 2 of the act, which forced states to create majority-minority districts so Black and brown voters had a fair chance to elect their ideal candidates to Congress, was unnecessary. This was the last standing major plank of the VRA after the court’s elimination of Section 5 in 2013’s Shelby County v. Holder. Like Chief Justice John Roberts’ majority opinion in Shelby, Justice Samuel Alito’s opinion in Callais was premised on the false narrative that racism is no longer a significant problem in America. “The Voting Rights Act led to ‘great strides’ in the ensuing decades: ‘voting tests were abolished, disparities in voter registration and turnout due to race was erased, and African Americans attained political office in record numbers,’ ” Alito wrote, justifying the nail in the coffin for the VRA. This is a willfully ignorant reading of the current reality of the country’s electoral politics, but Anthony Liuzzo was unsurprised by it. “Nobody should be surprised that the Supreme Court did this,” he said. “It was initiated a long time ago to try to get rid of the Voting Rights Act, and they’ve accomplished it—temporarily.” If his mother were still alive today, he says, she would be out on the streets, encouraging every eligible American to exercise their right to vote, in spite of Callais. This is how, he believes, this setback can be overcome. “They need to get off their behinds and get in the voting booth and vote. It’s the only way we’re going to turn this around now,” he said. “It’s going to be harder because we don’t have the guidelines or protections that the Voting Rights Act had given us, or that my mother’s blood gave us.” Dennis Dahmer, who is 73, similarly understands the stakes of what the court has done by rolling back key protections the VRA established. His father, Vernon Dahmer, was “a card-toting member of the NAACP,” he said. “As a result of that, there were a lot of whites who didn’t like that—at all.” Living in Mississippi, Vernon Dahmer watched his Black neighbors struggle to vote, impeded by poll taxes designed to target them. He decided to put his money where his mouth was and released a radio ad announcing that he would pay the poll tax for any Black American who wanted to register to vote but didn’t have the means to do so. The move angered local KKK members, who began plotting against Dahmer. One night around 2 a.m., two cars filled with armed KKK members rolled up to the Dahmers’ home. “They’re shooting in the house, shooting the windows out, throwing gallons of gas through the windows and torches behind it,” Dennis Dahmer said. “My dad is running through the house, from different doors and windows, shooting back at these carloads of klansmen.” Though Vernon Dahmer managed to survive the night, he died the next day from smoke inhalation that caused fatal lung damage. He was survived by his wife and seven children. His death energized Mississippi residents and prompted Johnson to wire his condolences to the family. The Callais opinion was the final act of the Roberts court’s decadeslong effort to roll back critical protections in the VRA that gave minority voters a discernible voice in Congress through representatives they hand-selected. In Shelby County, the justices had eliminated the guidance that jurisdictions with a history of voter suppression get Justice Department approval for new district maps and other electoral changes. Then, in 2021, through Brnovich v. Democratic National Committee, the court found that state laws that restricted ballots in a way that disproportionately affected minority voters were perfectly lawful. Finally, with Callais, the court’s conservative majority eviscerated Section 2, considered the heart of the act, which had forced states to create majority-minority districts in their congressional maps. “And in that way it greenlights redistricting plans that will disable minority communities—in Louisiana and across the Nation—from electing, as many majority communities can, ‘representatives of their choice,’ ” Justice Elena Kagan argued in her dissent. Dennis Dahmer believes that his father would be “highly pissed off at Americans who are not voting, because that is the problem.” He said, “We are in the situation we are today in America because of voter apathy.” Although state legislatures and the Trump administration actively push for measures that further suppress voter turnout, from race-based redistricting to the SAVE Act, the biggest tool we have left to fight back is our vote, he says. The failure to use that power is what is costing us our constitutional protections. “That is how they win elections, by keeping people away from the polls who will not vote for their agenda,” Dahmer said. Some 60 years ago, Andrew Goodman understood how a complicit government could actively suppress Black voters. “The Senators could not persist in this polite debate over the future dignity of a human race if the white Northerners were not so shockingly apathetic,” he wrote in a school paper, criticizing the North’s total accommodation of the segregationist South of the 1960s. Within days of penning those words, Goodman received an invitation to join a voter-registration project in Mississippi by the Student Nonviolent Coordinating Committee, a powerful civil rights group built up by young people pushing for racial equality. “He wanted to do something about it rather than just talk about it,” David Goodman, his younger brother, told me. Soon enough, Andrew Goodman was on his way to Mississippi to help register Black Americans to vote. His efforts were tragically cut short. On June 21, 1964, his first day in Mississippi, alongside Chaney and Schwerner, Goodman was murdered by the KKK in a brutal ambush that resulted in a 44-day-long search for their bodies. David Goodman believes that the U.S. is currently facing a struggle similar to the one his brother fought six decades ago, with the people in power attempting to take away Americans’ constitutional rights in order to expand their control. “They’re going to chip away at it in many, many, many different ways,” he said. “One way is to say that the Voting Rights Act is not valid and, furthermore, talk about methods and ways to gerrymander states so that Black people are outvoted by white people.” One of the simplest and most immediate steps Americans can take regain control of their rights is to simply vote and force accountability on politicians. “Otherwise, you sit there and moan and groan and don’t vote. They’re going to do what they want,” David Goodman said. American democracy has entered one of its darkest periods, but it’s one we have faced before. The Civil War tore the country apart but eventually led to the abolishment of slavery. The Jim Crow Era ushered in a new kind of slavery but eventually galvanized Congress to establish some of its most impactful legislation, in the VRA and the Civil Rights Act. “You go forward a little bit in a positive way, then get pushed backwards again, but this is the story of America,” concluded David Goodman. The unfortunate reality is that nearly every period of injustice the U.S. has experienced has come at the expense of human lives. The Liuzzo, Dahmer, and Goodman families know this all too well. The only way to sufficiently honor these legacies, alongside thousands of others who similarly participated in the civil rights era, is to pick up where they left off and continue their fight for equal rights and democracy for all. Donald Trump Is Reportedly Furious Right Now. I Can See Why.
2026-07-25

Donald Trump Is Reportedly Furious Right Now. I Can See Why.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-07-25

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Surge, the newsletter that covers the most important political nonsense of the week, delivered to your inbox every Saturday. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, which figures we must be living in truly dark times if even a trip to Taco Bell can make you feel gross afterward. This week: Maine Democrats have a new Senate nominee; just be sure to wear a helmet if you get near him. We have a brief couple of weeks to make jokes about the candidates in South Carolina’s quickie Republican Senate primary, and we intend to use them. Ukraine has picked up a powerful new ally in an internet weirdo who has Donald Trump’s ear. Seriously, though. Speaking of Trump, we are going old-fashioned in this edition, with a double-barrel start on the big guy and his questionable foreign policy decisions. The recently resumed hostilities with Iran are not going well. Four U.S. service members were killed this week, bringing the total to 18 since the war began. (Did you hear that, Department of Defense? It’s 18.) The U.S. has bombed Iranian targets for the last two weeks to no apparent change in Iran’s negotiating posture. The Houthis have saddled up to threaten shipping through the Red Sea, while the Strait of Hormuz remains a mess. Oil prices have shot back up since the end of the ceasefire, which affects a lot more than the price of gasoline. MAGA voters continue to sour on the conflict. The war is also expensive. All of this is making Trump incredibly mad, since there’s no clear way to get out of it absent the deployment of ground troops. As one source told Politico this week, “The president is now fully aware that the only way to the victory that he wants is completely politically impossible. The American people just will not support the kind of escalation it would take at this point.” We don’t mean to neener-neener the president at a delicate and dangerous moment. But this is yet another time for a reminder that if you’re thinking about starting a war, it’s wise to get buy-in from important stakeholders such as the U.S. Congress, the American people, or any of your allies. Anyone, really. To the extent that you were already not familiar: Our man wakes up every day and wings it with whatever international chess pieces are available to him. This week, Trump announced and his administration signed a deal that would supply Saudi Arabia with nuclear energy technology. It was a big win for Saudi Arabia and Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman, whose goal is to diversify the kingdom’s economy beyond oil. Here’s what the U.S. got in return: … Hold on, we’re just looking it up still … Hmm, alas, we’re not sure what the U.S. got in return. Potential nuclear proliferation in the Middle East? Is that something we wanted? The following day, however, Trump posted on social media that finalization of the deal was “totally subject to Saudi Arabia joining the very respected and successful Abraham Accords,” i.e., normalizing relations with Israel. But Saudi Arabia won’t strike a deal to normalize relations with Israel unless Israel works toward creating a Palestinian state, and we all know Israel’s current interest level in doing that. What happened here? Did Israel complain to Trump about the nuclear deal and Trump stood down? Isn’t Trump pissed at Israel these days, though? No one knows. It is all being done minute by minute, with no overarching strategy, based on whichever person calls him last before he smashes Send on those morning Truth Social posts. The Maine Democratic Party will hold its convention to nominate a replacement for Graham Platner in the Senate race this weekend. When the convention was first announced, it sounded as if it might be a thrilling opportunity to see real wheeling and dealing on a convention floor from which a nominee would eventually emerge. Nope! Most of the delegates elected to the convention last weekend support former state Senate President Troy Jackson, and all of his meaningful rivals quickly dropped out. Jackson, who lost the Democratic primary for governor earlier this year, is a logger from far northern Aroostook County whose views turned more progressive as time went on. In the build-up to the convention, though, the Washington Post reported on some stories from Maine that had been bubbling up on social media about his temper and shouting matches with fellow lawmakers. Most notably, he “once angrily threw a water bottle in a meeting after he couldn’t persuade a lawmaker to vote his way.” We don’t condone bottle-throwing in meetings. It’s certainly rude. We also don’t really—what’s the word?—care. No more nominee takebacks, Maine Democrats. This is your guy. House Republicans this week passed the framework for a third reconciliation bill—which bypasses the 60-vote Senate filibuster rule—of Donald Trump’s presidency. This framework, once fleshed out, would offer up to $73 billion for the military and intelligence services to cover the Iran war, $12 billion to bail out farmers screwed over by Trump’s trade policies, and $10 billion in incentives to states to offer some of the election reforms from the SAVE America Act, the GOP’s flagship voting bill that remains mega-dead in the Senate. Well, this reconciliation framework is likely mega-dead in the Senate too. There are not at this moment 50 Republican senators who want to vote on big bucks for the dreadfully unpopular Iran war or elements of the SAVE Act, and Republicans certainly don’t want to vote on unlimited Democratic amendments in a campaign season. Senate Majority Leader John Thune isn’t concealing his lack of interest in what the House is offering, saying that he wants to focus on government funding instead. In a White House press briefing on Thursday, press secretary Karoline Leavitt said that Trump’s “patience is running out” with the Senate. In response to this, Thune told reporters, “Maybe she or somebody else ought to get on the phone and get the votes.” Everything’s going great. Move over, Maine Democrats: It’s South Carolina’s turn for a quickie Senate primary that everyone jumps into. Candidate filing opened this week for the Republican primary to replace deceased Sen. Lindsey Graham on November’s ballot. The primary will be held Aug. 11 with a potential runoff on Aug. 25. It took many by surprise, then, when Lindsey Graham’s sister, Darline Graham, decided this week to run for a full term, even though she was considered to be a caretaker for the seat until the end of the year. She has Trump’s endorsement. But no one really knows anything about Darline Graham, so a few others are deciding to give it a shot. Rep. Russell Fry has entered the race, and why not? He’s allowed to keep running in his House reelection campaign concurrently, so it’s a free roll at a Senate seat. Rep. Ralph Norman did his best impression of a Maine Democrat by entering the race after losing his gubernatorial primary earlier in the year, and the candidate that Lindsey Graham defeated in his primary earlier this year, businessman Mark Lynch, has also joined. But—God bless—our favorite entrant into the race is Mark Sanford, the former governor whose political trajectory collapsed when he was busted visiting Argentina to see his mistress. Since that scandal, he has since served in his old House seat again, gotten primaried out of it for not appreciating Donald Trump enough, and run for president for a few months in the 2020 cycle, among other reported activities. Hopefully the primary is venomous. We could all use a little pick-me-up. The New York City mayor promised during his 2025 campaign that he would order the New York Police Department to arrest Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu if he stepped foot in the city. With Netanyahu’s annual trip to the United Nations General Assembly in September looming, it was time for the mayor to decide whether he would actually follow through with this. In a video he released this week, he announced he would not. “My administration has reviewed every avenue available, under applicable law, to determine whether New York City could execute the International Criminal Court’s arrest warrant if Benjamin Netanyahu came here,” he said. “It is clear that we do not have the independent legal authority to enforce this warrant.” No kidding. May we ask: Did anyone really believe he would follow through on this bit of aspirational rhetoric? Did anyone genuinely think that he would send a couple of cops from the night beat to arrest a foreign head of state visiting the U.N., under a warrant from an international body of justice that the United States doesn’t recognize? It was about as fantastical as believing that Trump would have Mexico pay to build the U.S. border wall. There’s no reason for Mamdani’s supporters to be disappointed, because it was never a serious idea to begin with. The conspiratorial MAGA influencer (and thus close confidante of Trump) has changed. Like most of her ilk, she was exceptionally critical of Ukraine and President Volodymyr Zelensky for years during the war, saying the country was full of “Nazi apologists” and calling Zelensky a “jihadi apologist.” This week, however, she visited Ukraine, interviewed Zelensky, and changed her tune on the conflict. She is now staunchly pro-Ukraine and observed, “I feel like such an asshole for minimizing the struggle of Ukrainians for the last 5 years,” explaining, “We have been so propagandized by Russia and we don’t even realize it.” (A lot of people realized that the right-wing influencers had been propagandized by Russia.) This is all well and good. Hopefully it makes some difference in right-wing politics and, more importantly, Trump’s view of the conflict. But—now it’s our turn to play conspiracy theorist—it’s also terribly difficult to take her conversion at face value. How does someone who’s built her substantial MAGA following and income in part by checking the “Hate Ukraine” box suddenly get on a trip to Kyiv to interview all of its powerful people and announce that she had been wrong all along? We’re talking about a complete 180. Loomer, for what it’s worth, has denied that she was paid by the Ukrainian government for the trip. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The 1867 Case That Allowed Ordinary Parents—Not Judges—to Redefine the Constitution.
2026-07-25

The 1867 Case That Allowed Ordinary Parents—Not Judges—to Redefine the Constitution.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-07-25

Visit Original Website
When freedom was snatched away, Elizabeth Turner’s mother fought back, and changed the Constitution. Please enable javascript to get your Slate Plus feeds. If you can't access your feeds, please contact customer support. Thanks! Check your phone for a link to finish setting up your feed. Enter your phone number and we'll text you a link to set up the podcast in your app: We'll only text you about setting up this podcast, no spam. Apple Podcasts will only work on MacOS operating systems since Catalina. We do not support Android apps on desktop at this time. These links will only work if you're on the device you listen to podcasts on. We're sorry, but something went wrong while fetching your podcast feeds. Please contact us at plus@slate.com for help. In 1867, an 8-year-old girl named Elizabeth Turner became the epicenter of a legal battle that would help define what freedom really meant after the Civil War. Days after Maryland abolished slavery, her former enslaver had her re-indentured under a sham “apprenticeship” law used to seize Black children. Elizabeth’s mother—and thousands of other parents—refused to accept it. In this second episode of By the People, legal scholar Lyle Cherneff guides Dahlia Lithwick and Mark Joseph Stern through a Union Army general’s archive of letters that documented the plight of Black parents who petitioned, marched, and filed habeas corpus claims to win their children back. These parents read the new constitutional guarantees of freedom for themselves, and demanded they be enforced, long before any Supreme Court told them what those words meant. The resulting case, In re: Turner, helped end the practice of racialized child “apprenticeship” and shaped the meaning of the 13th and 14th amendments. But the parents who fought for it were nearly written out of the story. In this episode, we seek to restore that record, tracing a direct line from their fight to ordinary Americans’ resistance to ICE raids today. By the People is Slate’s end-of-term project exploring how ordinary Americans have shaped the meaning of the Constitution outside the Supreme Court. You can read the full package of articles accompanying this series at slate.com/bythepeople The justices publish their opinions a few dozen times a year. Dahlia and Mark share theirs every week. Slate Plus gets you all of it: Amicus ad-free with a weekly bonus segment, and no paywalls anywhere on Slate. Join now. Visit slate.com/amicusplus to get access wherever you listen. Or, you can subscribe directly from the Amicus show page on Apple Podcasts and Spotify. The smokeless cigar bar heads to New Jersey for a look at a radical decision striking down the state’s assault-rifle ban—and the implications for the upcoming SCOTUS term. A show about the law and the nine Supreme Court justices who interpret it for the rest of America. Dahlia Lithwick writes about the courts and the law for Slate and hosts the podcast Amicus. Mark Joseph Stern is a Slate senior writer.
